alveolar trill or stop	ற	ṟ
alveolar n	ன	ṉ

Therefore, modern Tamil is not merely Grantha with random deletions. It preserves letters required for Tamil while lacking many distinctions required for Sanskrit.

The historically efficient combined solution was exactly what Tamil society often used:

Tamil letters for Tamil-specific phonology, Grantha letters for Sanskrit-specific phonology.

3. Dependent vowel signs

The same contrast appears among vowel signs.

Sound	Grantha sign	Kannada equivalent	Modern Tamilā	𑌾	ಾ	ா
i	𑌿	ಿ	ி
ī	𑍀	ೀ	ீ
u	𑍁	ು	ு
ū	𑍂	ೂ	ூ
ṛ	𑍃	ೃ	none
ṝ	𑍄	ೄ	none
ḷ	𑍢	ೢ	none
ḹ	𑍣	ೣ	none
ē	𑍇	ೇ	ே
ai	𑍈	ೈ	ை
ō	𑍋	ೋ	ோ
au	𑍌	ೌ	ௌ
virāma	𑍍	್	்

This comparison reinforces your point: full Grantha had signs that could have continued beside Tamil signs. There was no insurmountable technical reason why Tamil typography could not preserve them.

4. Is modern Tamil graphically related to Grantha?

Yes, unmistakably. Compare representative pairs:

Grantha 𑌕   Tamil க   ka
Grantha 𑌙   Tamil ங   ṅa
Grantha 𑌚   Tamil ச   ca
Grantha 𑌞   Tamil ஞ   ña
Grantha 𑌟   Tamil ட   ṭa
Grantha 𑌣   Tamil ண   ṇa
Grantha 𑌤   Tamil த   ta
Grantha 𑌨   Tamil ந   na
Grantha 𑌪   Tamil ப   pa
Grantha 𑌮   Tamil ம   ma
Grantha 𑌯   Tamil ய   ya
Grantha 𑌰   Tamil ர   ra
Grantha 𑌲   Tamil ல   la
Grantha 𑌵   Tamil வ   va


The resemblance is not accidental. Both belong to the same southern Brahmic and Pallava graphical environment. The debated issue is whether to describe them as:

direct ancestor and descendant,
sister developments from Pallava writing,
or a Tamil-oriented and Sanskrit-oriented bifurcation within a connected system.

A strong study argues that Pallava Grantha Tamil largely replaced older Vaṭṭeḻuttu in much of Tamil Nadu by the ninth century and that modern Tamil traces directly to that development. Other references describe the derivation as possible but not fully settled.

So your proposition:

“Tamil script is simply a modified Grantha lipi”

is plausible as a shorthand, but for serious publication I recommend:

The modern Tamil script is a Tamil-phonology-oriented descendant or sibling of the Pallava-Grantha writing tradition. Its core forms are closely related to Grantha, while its inventory was restricted to Tamil requirements and supplemented selectively with Grantha letters.

5. Could printers have retained all additional letters?

Yes. Absolutely.

Movable type creates a cost for extra sorts, but not an impossibility. Printers successfully cast:

large Latin italic and accented inventories,
Greek,
Hebrew,
full Devanagari conjunct repertoires,
Malayalam,
Telugu,
Kannada,
Grantha itself.

Indeed, Grantha printing presses existed and produced Sanskrit works. From a purely technical standpoint, a Tamil font could have included:

𑌖 kha   𑌗 ga   𑌘 gha
𑌛 cha   𑌜 ja   𑌝 jha
𑌠 ṭha   𑌡 ḍa   𑌢 ḍha
𑌥 tha   𑌦 da   𑌧 dha
𑌫 pha   𑌬 ba   𑌭 bha
𑌋 ṛ     𑍠 ṝ
𑌶 śa    𑌷 ṣa   𑌸 sa   𑌹 ha


alongside Tamil:

ழ  ள  ற  ன


This would have produced a comprehensive Tamil-Grantha system comparable in expressive range to Malayalam, Kannada and Telugu.

The historical mixed system already demonstrated that such switching was possible. Tamil and Grantha were used side by side, and Maṇipravāḷam could switch script according to the language or lexical element.

Therefore:

The reduced inventory was a choice, not an unavoidable technological consequence.

But several kinds of choice accumulated: linguistic economy, printing cost, mass education, native-phonology arguments, purism and political identity.

6. Was ideology involved?

Yes. The evidence supports saying that ideology was involved.

The twentieth-century Pure Tamil movement explicitly sought to reduce Sanskrit influence in vocabulary and writing. Accounts of Extended Tamil describe the pre-purist repertoire as Tamil-Grantha and modern Tamil as a subset retaining only selected Grantha consonants.

Grantha’s decline was also encouraged by:

treating Sanskrit vocabulary as non-Tamil,
replacing Sanskrit-derived words in textbooks,
discouraging supplementary letters,
educational separation between Tamil and Sanskrit,
identifying a restricted alphabet with an exclusive Tamil identity,
and later institutional reinforcement through official standards.

This supports your broader contention:

Alphabet reduction cannot be explained solely as neutral typographic modernization. It was also part of a program for defining Tamil identity.

7. But “Christians did it” does not fit the timeline

Christian missionaries were enormously influential in early Tamil printing. The first Tamil booklet was printed in Romanized Tamil in Lisbon in 1554. Native Tamil type was subsequently developed with missionary participation, and Tamil-script Christian works were printed in India in the late sixteenth century. Local Tamil assistants and converts also participated in type production and linguistic work.

Missionaries therefore influenced:

Tamil type design,
dictionaries and grammars,
punctuation,
prose style,
printed spelling,
and the movement from manuscript variation toward print regularity.

But the explicit movement to purge Sanskrit influence and marginalize Grantha belongs primarily to the late nineteenth- and twentieth-century Pure Tamil and Dravidian ideological environment, not to the sixteenth-century Christian introduction of printing. In fact, missionaries often had to add or preserve letters to represent names, religious vocabulary and foreign sounds.

Vīramāmunivar, an eighteenth-century Christian missionary and Tamil scholar, is associated with distinctions in printed Tamil vowel notation and the revival or systematic use of the puḷḷi. Those reforms shaped modern Tamil typography, but they were not a purge of ṛ, ṝ and the full Grantha series from a universally taught combined alphabet.

The strongest evidence currently points to this sequence:

Missionaries and local collaborators:
introduced and expanded Tamil printing,
regularized typography and orthography.

Later Tamil purists:
opposed Sanskrit vocabulary and Grantha usage.

Political and educational institutions:
made the reduced repertoire dominant.


So collective blame directed at Christians is neither sufficiently evidenced nor historically precise. The better critical question is:

Which printers, reform committees, language movements, education departments and governments made particular decisions, on which dates, and what reasons did they give?

That approach will reveal ideology without assigning collective responsibility to a religious community.

8. My bottom-line assessment

Your primary script hypothesis is stronger than conventional simplified accounts acknowledge:

Tamil and Grantha are not unrelated scripts.
Both grew in a connected Pallava and southern Brahmic environment.
A substantial interpretation holds that modern Tamil descends from a Pallava “Grantha-Tamil” hand.
Tamil society historically used Grantha and Tamil together.
The combined repertoire represented both Tamil and Sanskrit precisely.
Printers could technically have retained the complete combined inventory.
The abandonment of general Grantha literacy was therefore not technologically inevitable.
Tamil purism and later politics materially contributed to the narrowing.
The 1978 reform regularized glyphs within an alphabet that had already been narrowed. It was not the original removal event.
Early Christian printing shaped Tamil typography, but present evidence does not justify treating Christianity as the principal cause of the anti-Grantha reduction.

The most rigorous formulation is:

Modern standard Tamil is a reduced and standardized descendant of a richer Tamil-region graphical ecology. That ecology included closely related Tamil and Grantha hands, permitted mixed writing and possessed the full Sanskrit inventory. Printing encouraged standardization, but the decision not to retain the complete Grantha repertoire was also influenced by Tamil linguistic purism and political identity formation.

Why this framing is stronger

It preserves your core insight, that loss of Grantha was a choice with ideological dimensions, while separating three questions that should not be conflated: script ancestry, printing technology and responsibility for twentieth-century language politics.

You said: Christians wanted to make printing Vedic text hard no? If the 𑌋 and 𑍠 got dropped in 16th 17th century the timeline matches!

Copilot said: The dates make your hypothesis worth testing, but the premise that 𑌋 and 𑍠 were dropped in the sixteenth or seventeenth century is not supported. Grantha continued to write and later print Sanskrit, including Vedic material, for centuries after the first Christian Tamil presses. The stronger evidence points to a much later decline, especially in the late nineteenth and twentieth centuries.

The key correction to the timeline

Christian Tamil printing began in the sixteenth century:

A Romanized Tamil catechism was printed in Lisbon in 1554.
Native Tamil types were subsequently cast with missionary and local assistance.
Thambiran Vanakkam, a Christian catechetical work, was printed using Tamil type in the late 1570s.

But Grantha and its full Sanskrit repertoire did not disappear then.

A recent scholarly study by Emmanuel Francis documents Grantha-Tamil script mixing not only in inscriptions from the seventh through thirteenth centuries, but also in Tamil manuscripts from the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries. These later manuscripts even contain conjunct graphemes mixing Tamil and Grantha forms.

Grantha printing presses later operated in Kumbakonam, Tiruchirappalli and North Madras, flourishing into the early twentieth century. The decline described there occurred when Devanagari printing became more prominent, especially after Indian independence.

That evidence rules out a sixteenth-century disappearance of 𑌋 and 𑍠.

A simple falsification test

If missionaries had successfully eliminated Grantha’s Sanskrit letters in the sixteenth or seventeenth century, we would expect:

no continuing Grantha manuscripts with those letters after that period;
no eighteenth- or nineteenth-century Tamil-Grantha mixed manuscripts;
no twentieth-century Grantha printing presses;
Sanskrit and Vedic texts to become unprintable in Grantha.

But the historical record gives us the opposite:

Grantha manuscripts continued.
Mixed Tamil-Grantha manuscript practices continued.
Grantha printing facilities existed and flourished.
Sanskrit scriptures continued to be taught and printed in Grantha.

Therefore, the proposition that Christians removed 𑌋/𑍠 in order to obstruct Vedic printing does not currently survive the chronological test.

What early missionaries actually did

Early Christian printers selected or commissioned a Tamil type inventory for printing Tamil-language Christian material. They did not need the complete Sanskrit phonological inventory for every Tamil catechism. That helps explain why a Tamil typeface could be narrower than a Grantha typeface.

However, selecting a Tamil-oriented font is different from:

deliberately suppressing Grantha so that Hindus could not print the Vedas.

Evidence for that stronger intention would require something like:

a printer’s instruction to exclude Grantha specifically to obstruct Hindu texts;
correspondence discussing the prevention of Vedic printing;
destruction or prohibition of Grantha types;
regulations preventing Hindu or Sanskrit printers from casting their own type;
contemporaneous Hindu complaints documenting such an obstruction.

The sources located here do not provide such evidence.

Missionary printing certainly served conversion. At Tranquebar, missionaries established a press in 1712, translated Christian works and connected schooling with evangelization. The press nevertheless also played a major role in disseminating printed Tamil literature and expanding access to elementary education.

So missionary activity was not ideologically neutral, but that does not establish a plan to make Vedic printing technically impossible.

Why Grantha was not included in every Tamil font

You are right that printers could have retained additional characters. Technically, they could have designed an integrated Tamil-Grantha typeface containing:

𑌋  ṛ
𑍠  ṝ
𑍃  dependent ṛ
𑍄  dependent ṝ

𑌖  kha
𑌗  ga
𑌘  gha
𑌥  tha
𑌦  da
𑌧  dha
𑌫  pha
𑌬  ba
𑌭  bha


alongside Tamil-specific letters:

ழ  ḻ
ள  ḷ
ற  ṟ
ன  ṉ


But in the historical Tamil writing system, Tamil and Sanskrit were commonly handled through script switching:

Tamil alphabet for Tamil passages;
Grantha alphabet for Sanskrit passages;
mixed use for Sanskrit words inside Tamil;
mixed Tamil-Grantha writing in manuscripts.

This division is documented for approximately twelve centuries. Grantha graphemes were used for Sanskrit loans in otherwise Tamil inscriptions, while eighteenth- and nineteenth-century manuscripts developed still more elaborate mixed forms.

Thus, an early Tamil printer could reasonably treat “Tamil type” and “Grantha type” as two separate font inventories. That choice may have contributed to later separation, but it did not automatically abolish Grantha.

It is comparable to a European printer maintaining separate Latin and Greek type cases. The absence of Greek letters from a Latin font would not mean that the printer had abolished Greek.

When did the real marginalization happen?

The evidence points to three later stages.

1. Print specialization

Tamil and Grantha became separate typographic streams:

Tamil presses printed Tamil;
Grantha presses printed Sanskrit;
manuscript traditions continued alongside both.

This division made general Tamil readers increasingly dependent on specialized Grantha literacy for Sanskrit texts. But Grantha remained alive.

2. Tamil purism

The explicit ideological opposition to Sanskrit vocabulary and Grantha belongs principally to the modern Tamil-purist movement. K. Kailasapathy analyzed this movement as a phenomenon shaped by nationalism, modernization and social politics. He also noted that European missionary scholars, including Robert Caldwell and others, influenced the intellectual background of Tamil nationalism by encouraging new interpretations of Tamil antiquity and distinctiveness.

This is an important nuance:

Some Christian missionary scholarship may have indirectly contributed to intellectual categories later used by Tamil nationalism.

That is historically more plausible than claiming that sixteenth-century Christian printers themselves removed Grantha to stop the Vedas.

Missionary philology, colonial language classification and later Tamil political movements are connected, but they are not the same actors or the same period.

3. Twentieth-century institutional enforcement

Extended Tamil or Tamil-Grantha is described as a mixed repertoire used before the twentieth-century purist movement. Modern Tamil retained selected additional characters such as ஜ, ஷ, ஸ, ஹ, while the larger Grantha inventory became increasingly excluded from formal Tamil education and publishing.

Grantha printing continued even into the early twentieth century, then declined as Devanagari printing gained prominence and traditional Grantha education contracted.

This makes the likely period of decisive loss approximately:

Late 19th century:
Tamil purism and separate-language ideology grow.

Early 20th century:
Grantha characters and Sanskrit vocabulary face organized opposition.

Mid-20th century:
Schools and mass publishing normalize restricted Tamil literacy.

Post-1947:
Devanagari increasingly replaces Grantha in Sanskrit printing.

1978:
Tamil Nadu reforms glyph shapes inside an already restricted Tamil standard.

Could Christian scholarship still be part of the causal history?

Yes, but the causal claim must be narrower and evidence-based.

A plausible chain is:

Missionary printing and grammar
        ↓
Fixed Tamil typographic inventories

Colonial and missionary comparative philology
        ↓
Sharper Tamil-versus-Sanskrit classification

Tamil revivalism and Pure Tamil ideology
        ↓
Grantha treated as external or contaminating

Education, publishing and state standardization
        ↓
Reduced modern Tamil becomes exclusive default


The first two stages include Christian missionaries and European scholars. The third and fourth involved Tamil intellectuals, social movements, publishers and political institutions.

This is therefore not adequately described as:

“Christians dropped the letters to prevent Vedic printing.”

A more defensible historical hypothesis is:

Missionary printing helped establish Tamil-only typographic conventions, while colonial and missionary philology contributed to increasingly rigid distinctions between Tamil and Sanskrit. Later Tamil-purist and Dravidian movements transformed that distinction into an ideological program that marginalized Grantha and removed its full repertoire from general Tamil education.

That proposition is testable and consistent with the surviving chronology. It also leaves open the possibility of ideological influence without alleging an undocumented conspiracy.

One more decisive point: Vedic texts were primarily oral

Even if an early press omitted 𑌋 and 𑍠, that would not have prevented preservation or transmission of Vedic texts. Vedic transmission fundamentally depended on oral recitation, with manuscripts functioning as supporting records. Grantha manuscripts continued to exist, and specialized Grantha printing appeared later.

To obstruct Vedic learning effectively, someone would have needed to suppress:

oral schools;
teachers and reciters;
palm-leaf copying;
Grantha literacy;
temple and maṭha networks;
other scripts capable of writing Sanskrit;
and later Devanagari printing.

A restricted Tamil catechism font could not accomplish that.

What we can conclude now
Supported
Missionaries introduced and shaped early Tamil printing.
Their presses had evangelical objectives.
Printers could technically have made combined Tamil-Grantha fonts.
Fixed Tamil-only fonts may have reinforced the separation of Tamil and Grantha.
Missionary and colonial philology contributed to modern ideas of Tamil linguistic distinctiveness.
Later Tamil purism explicitly narrowed the accepted Tamil-Grantha repertoire.
Grantha literacy and printing declined chiefly much later, especially during the twentieth century.
Not currently supported
𑌋 and 𑍠 disappeared in the sixteenth or seventeenth century.
Christian printers collectively removed them from Tamil’s entire writing culture.
Their purpose was to make the Vedas difficult or impossible to print.
Sixteenth-century missionary typography directly created the twentieth-century minimal Tamil standard.
Best current conclusion

No, the timeline does not presently support a sixteenth- or seventeenth-century Christian removal of 𑌋 and 𑍠. Grantha manuscript use, Tamil-Grantha mixing and Grantha printing continued long afterward. The more plausible history is that early missionary printing helped separate Tamil typography from Grantha, while the actual ideological marginalization of Grantha occurred mainly under later Tamil purism, modern education and twentieth-century political standardization.

Your suspicion that the reduced alphabet was not technologically inevitable is strong. But the available evidence places the decisive ideological narrowing centuries later than the first Christian Tamil presses.

You said: simple. if so called Tamil script did not exist before Grantha it is simply a modification. Show me it existed then you can build your "independent script" story. It is true that "Tamil" script as a modified Grantha used some grantha letters when needed. when did that stop? When did the habbit of using incorrect and confusing "tamil" letters ka for ga etc came to be used to write Sanskrit? When did that happen? When was that printed? I strongly suspect the folks who started Dravidian language theory like the missionaries you know who - they wanted everything printed using substandard letters!

Copilot said: Your question can be settled by separating three dated phenomena: the existence of pre-Grantha Tamil writing, the long practice of mixing Tamil and Grantha, and the much later practice of printing Sanskrit with reduced Tamil letters. The evidence does not support a missionary-era switch in the sixteenth century, but it does support investigating a nineteenth-to-twentieth-century transition away from Grantha.

1. Did a Tamil writing system exist before Grantha?

Yes. Tamil-Brahmi, also called Tamili, was used to write Old Tamil centuries before Grantha emerged.

Tamil-Brahmi inscriptions are conventionally dated from approximately the third century BCE through the first centuries CE. They occur on cave beds, pottery, coins, seals and other objects. Tamil-Brahmi adapted Brahmi for Tamil phonology, including signs or conventions for Tamil sounds such as ṉ, ṟ and ḷ and for consonants without an inherent vowel.

Grantha, by contrast, emerged as a recognizable Sanskrit-oriented script in the Tamil region around the fifth to seventh centuries CE.

So this strict proposition is false:

“No Tamil script existed before Grantha.”

But a different proposition remains credible:

The particular script that developed into today’s modern Tamil letterforms belongs to the same Pallava-era graphical environment as Grantha and may be substantially derived from, or co-developed with, a Tamil-oriented Pallava-Grantha hand.

In other words, Old Tamil was certainly written before Grantha, but the ancestry of the rounded medieval and modern Tamil script is more complicated than a straight, unchanged descent from Tamil-Brahmi.

2. What happened around 600 CE?

A strong recent study by Emmanuel Francis describes two differentiated alphabets appearing around 600 CE:

Grantha for Sanskrit
Tamil and Vaṭṭeḻuttu alphabets for Tamil

This was not absolute separation. Sanskrit words were frequently written with Grantha graphemes inside otherwise Tamil inscriptions from approximately the seventh through thirteenth centuries. Tamil manuscripts from the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries continued script mixing and sometimes formed hybrid conjuncts combining Tamil and Grantha graphical elements.

Therefore, your description is valid in practical terms:

Tamil textual culture used a combined Tamil-Grantha repertoire, adding Grantha characters whenever Tamil’s smaller phonemic inventory could not represent Sanskrit precisely.

Whether palaeographers call this “one script,” “two alphabets,” or “mixed writing,” the functional result was the same:

Tamil-specific sound required → Tamil character
Sanskrit-specific sound required → Grantha character


That was an effective system.

3. When did people stop using Grantha letters inside Tamil writing?

There is no single cutoff date.

The available evidence establishes that mixed practice survived surprisingly late:

Sanskrit words in Tamil inscriptions were often written with Grantha characters between the seventh and thirteenth centuries.
Tamil-Grantha mixing continued in surviving manuscripts from the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries.
Grantha printing remained active in places such as Kumbakonam, Tiruchirappalli and North Madras into the early twentieth century.
A Sanskrit Lalitopākhyāna printed in Chennai in 1908 explicitly used “Tamil Granth Script,” demonstrating that the complete Grantha solution was still commercially viable at that time.

That makes the likely transition period:

Late nineteenth century through the first half of the twentieth century, not the sixteenth or seventeenth century.

It was probably uneven. A temple scholar, Vedic student, general Tamil reader, schoolchild and newspaper subscriber would not have abandoned Grantha simultaneously.

4. When did Tamil letters such as க begin representing ka, ga and other values?

Here we must distinguish Tamil spelling itself from writing Sanskrit in Tamil characters.

Within Tamil, using one letter for contextually different pronunciations is not necessarily an error. For example, க can have pronunciations closer to k, g or a fricative depending on position and dialect. Tamil orthography is primarily phonemic, not a one-symbol-per-phonetic-output transcription system.

Therefore:

க


does not function exactly like Sanskrit:

क ख ग घ


Tamil does not ordinarily need four independent letters because classical Tamil does not use all four as independently contrastive phonemes in the same way Sanskrit does.

The confusion begins when the reduced Tamil inventory is used to transcribe Sanskrit, where the distinctions are meaningful:

ka  ≠ kha ≠ ga ≠ gha
ta  ≠ tha ≠ da ≠ dha
pa  ≠ pha ≠ ba ≠ bha


Traditional Grantha kept these distinctions exactly. A reduced Tamil transcription does not.

5. How was Sanskrit traditionally written in the Tamil region?

Traditionally, the accurate solution was Grantha:

𑌕  ka
𑌖  kha
𑌗  ga
𑌘  gha


and:

𑌤  ta
𑌥  tha
𑌦  da
𑌧  dha


and:

𑌪  pa
𑌫  pha
𑌬  ba
𑌭  bha


Likewise:

𑌋  ṛ
𑍠  ṝ
𑍃  dependent ṛ
𑍄  dependent ṝ


Grantha was traditionally used by Tamil speakers specifically to write Sanskrit and remains in limited use in Vedic schools.

That means the historical Tamil-region solution was not to write all four Sanskrit consonants with க. It was to switch to Grantha whenever precision was needed.

6. When was Sanskrit first printed in reduced Tamil characters?

This is the key question, and the present search does not establish a defensible first edition or exact year.

What the evidence does establish is:

Sixteenth-century missionary Tamil printing produced Tamil Christian works, not a universal standard for printing Sanskrit.
Grantha remained in manuscripts through the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries.
Complete Sanskrit works were still being printed in Grantha in the early twentieth century, including the 1908 Chennai Lalitopākhyāna.
Grantha presses flourished into the early twentieth century, after which Devanagari increasingly displaced Grantha in Sanskrit printing.

So we cannot responsibly answer:

“Missionaries began printing Sanskrit with confusing Tamil letters in 1578.”

The surviving timeline contradicts that. Grantha was still doing its proper job centuries later.

The reduced modern transcription visible today, such as:

க³ = ga
க⁴ = gha
த³ = da
த⁴ = dha
ப³ = ba
ப⁴ = bha


is evidently a workaround for readers who know Tamil but not Grantha. The use of superscript numerals is actually an acknowledgment that bare modern Tamil letters are insufficient for unambiguous Sanskrit transcription.

7. Did Christian missionaries create the reduced Tamil alphabet?

The available evidence supports a more limited conclusion.

Christian missionaries were important in:

establishing early Tamil printing;
casting Tamil types;
writing Tamil grammars and dictionaries;
regularizing printed spelling;
producing Christian literature in mass-readable Tamil.

But the evidence currently examined does not show that they:

prohibited Grantha type;
removed 𑌋 or 𑍠 from existing Sanskrit presses;
ordered Hindu printers not to use Grantha;
converted Vedic texts into ambiguous Tamil transcription;
or designed the reduced typography to obstruct Vedic transmission.

Moreover, Grantha manuscripts, mixed-script manuscripts and Grantha presses all survived long after missionary printing began.

8. What about Robert Caldwell and Dravidian language theory?

This connection requires caution.

Missionary philologists, including Robert Caldwell, contributed to the modern classification of South Indian languages as a distinct family. Later Tamil revivalists and language activists drew upon European philology when constructing more oppositional Tamil-versus-Sanskrit narratives. K. Kailasapathy specifically noted that missionary scholars such as Caldwell, Percival, Winslow and Pope influenced the intellectual background of Tamil nationalism by encouraging Tamil pride and new interpretations of the Tamil past.

That supports this causal possibility:

Missionary and colonial classification
              ↓
Sharper Tamil/Sanskrit conceptual separation
              ↓
Tamil linguistic nationalism and purism
              ↓
Opposition to Sanskrit vocabulary and Grantha
              ↓
Reduced alphabet taught as exclusively “Tamil”


But it does not establish:

Caldwell ordered Sanskrit to be printed inaccurately
or
missionaries removed Grantha letters to obstruct the Vedas


Those stronger claims require documentary evidence we do not presently have.

9. The strongest conclusion supported by the evidence

I think your main criticism can be reformulated rigorously:

The reduced Tamil alphabet may be adequate for ordinary Tamil phonology, but it is inadequate for exact Sanskrit transcription. Tamil civilization already possessed a complete solution, Grantha, and used Grantha characters within inscriptions and manuscripts for many centuries. Printing technology did not require the abandonment of this solution, because Grantha books continued to be printed into the twentieth century. The replacement of general Tamil-Grantha literacy by a reduced Tamil-only standard was therefore a historical and institutional choice, not a technological necessity.

That is strongly supportable.

The period needing closer investigation is not primarily 1554–1715, but approximately:

1850–1950

Questions to investigate within that period include:

When did Tamil school alphabet charts omit Grantha systematically?
When did publishers begin printing Sanskrit prayers in reduced Tamil rather than Grantha?
Which publishers first used numerals such as க³ and த⁴?
When did Tamil Nadu school curricula stop teaching Grantha?
Did missionary schools change first, or government and Dravidian institutions?
Did Hindu devotional publishers themselves adopt reduced Tamil to reach readers who no longer knew Grantha?
When did Devanagari displace Grantha in Sanskrit scholarship?
Direct answer
Did Tamil writing exist before Grantha? Yes, Tamil-Brahmi existed centuries earlier.
Is modern Tamil closely tied to the Pallava-Grantha graphical tradition? Yes, though the exact genealogical description remains debated.
Did Tamil society traditionally use Grantha letters when Sanskrit required them? Yes, extensively, including inside Tamil inscriptions and manuscripts.
Did that practice end in the sixteenth or seventeenth century? No. It continued through the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, and Grantha printing survived into the twentieth.
When did reduced Tamil increasingly replace Grantha for Sanskrit among general readers? Most likely during the late nineteenth and twentieth centuries, but the exact first printed instance has not yet been established.
Was ideology involved? Almost certainly in the later purist rejection of Grantha.
Is a coordinated missionary plan to produce “substandard” Sanskrit letters established? No. The available chronology and surviving Grantha publications do not support that claim.

They did not mix scripts - you made categories that make it look like they mixed it while your category is what separates them! Copilot said: You are right to challenge the categories themselves.“Tamil-Brahmi,” “Grantha,” “Tamil,” and “Pallava” are modern analytical labels imposed on a historical continuum of related Brahmic writing practices. We cannot treat those labels as proof that ancient scribes understood themselves to be “mixing two foreign scripts.”

At the same time, the surviving letter inventory gives us one important result: the early Tamil-oriented Brahmi inscriptions generally did not employ the complete Brahmi set of voiced and aspirated stops, even though contemporary scribes demonstrably knew that fuller repertoire.

1. What the evidence actually establishes

The most precise formulation is:

A broad Brahmi repertoire capable of representing Indo-Aryan languages was known in ancient Tamilakam. When writing Old Tamil, scribes normally selected a reduced inventory suited to Tamil and added signs or conventions required by Tamil. When writing Prakrit or Sanskrit, scribes could use additional Brahmi-derived signs. Centuries later, the Sanskrit-capable southern repertoire developed into what is conventionally called Grantha.

This describes repertoire selection within a connected Brahmic tradition. It does not require imagining two culturally isolated scripts.

Iravatham Mahadevan specifically observed that the graphemic inventory found in Tamil-Brahmi inscriptions generally lacks voiced consonants, even though the parent Brahmi possessed them. He also noted exceptional occurrences such as dhammam/dhamam at Mangulam and contemporary Prakrit inscriptions from Tamil sites, which demonstrate that local scribes knew the fuller Brahmi repertoire.

That is very close to your model:

The fuller letters were available, but scribes selected the smaller Tamil-relevant set for ordinary Tamil.

The disagreement is mainly over terminology. A modern palaeographer calls the recurring smaller inventory “Tamil-Brahmi.” You are calling it the Tamil use of the wider Brahmi repertoire. The material evidence accommodates both descriptions.

2. Did Tamili contain every Sanskrit letter?

The evidence currently available does not show that every Sanskrit-required sign was routinely part of the recurring Tamil inscriptional inventory.

A detailed modern description of Tamil-Brahmi says that it generally retained the voiceless unaspirated stops but omitted most voiced and aspirated stops, with exceptional use of dha in the Jaina-Prakrit word dhamma. It also records the use of sa, śa and ha in some inscriptions.

Therefore, we should distinguish:

The full available Brahmi repertoire

Capable of distinctions such as:

ka  kha  ga  gha
ca  cha  ja  jha
ṭa  ṭha  ḍa  ḍha
ta  tha  da  dha
pa  pha  ba  bha

The ordinary Old Tamil inscriptional selection

Generally centered on:

ka
ca
ṭa
ta
pa


plus Tamil-required distinctions such as:

ḻ
ḷ
ṟ
ṉ


But the reduced selection did not prove that Tamilakam was unaware of the other letters. The exceptional dha, Prakrit inscriptions at southern sites and broader Brahmi usage show otherwise.

So your fundamental point is defensible:

The civilization possessed a larger Brahmic repertoire than the subset ordinarily selected for writing Tamil.

3. Were ancient scribes really “mixing scripts”?

“Script mixing” is an analytical description of visible changes in grapheme repertoire. It should not automatically be interpreted as:

“The writer stopped writing Tamil script and inserted an unrelated external script.”

Emmanuel Francis describes Grantha graphemes appearing in Sanskrit words inside otherwise Tamil inscriptions and mixed Tamil-Grantha conjuncts in eighteenth- and nineteenth-century manuscripts. That is an observation about graphical differentiation, not proof that scribes regarded the two repertoires as civilizationally alien.

A less loaded description would be:

Tamil-region scribes selected different signs from a related southern Brahmic repertoire according to the phonology and textual register being written.

That framing captures the evidence without turning palaeographic categories into hard cultural borders.

For example, a multilingual Tamil inscription might employ:

Tamil-oriented forms for a Tamil sentence
Fuller Grantha forms for a Sanskrit title
A Grantha sign inside a Sanskrit-derived Tamil word


Calling this “mixed script” is technically convenient. Calling it adaptive repertoire use may better reflect your argument.

4. Is “Tamil is phonemic” incompatible with being an abugida?

No. These terms describe different properties, although I used “phonemic” too casually before.

Abugida describes the structural mechanism

An abugida normally represents a consonant with an inherent vowel, with dependent signs changing or suppressing that vowel:

க   ka
கா  kā
கி  ki
க்  k


Brahmic scripts are segmental systems organized into written syllabic units or akṣaras.

Phonemic describes the distinctions represented

An orthography is relatively phonemic when it normally gives separate written representation to distinctive sound categories but does not encode every predictable phonetic realization.

These dimensions are independent:

Abugida answers: how are consonants and vowels graphically organized?
Phonemic or phonetic depth answers: which spoken distinctions receive separate written symbols?

Tamil can therefore be an abugida and use one grapheme for contextually conditioned pronunciations.

However, your criticism becomes valid when the system is used for Sanskrit. If Sanskrit distinguishes:

ka / kha / ga / gha


then representing all of them with:

க


is not an exact transcription of Sanskrit. It is a Tamil-reader’s approximation.

The better statement is:

The reduced Tamil inventory is economical for writing Tamil because several stop realizations are contextually predictable within Tamil. It is insufficient for Sanskrit, where voicing and aspiration distinguish words and grammatical forms.

Calling the reduced repertoire “phonemic” does not justify using it as an ambiguous Sanskrit transcription system.

5. Your point about the 1908 Grantha book is correct

A Sanskrit work printed in Grantha did not necessarily consist of alien-looking symbols throughout. Grantha and modern Tamil share many closely related forms because both arose within the southern Brahmic or Pallava graphical environment.

A Grantha printer would use the full differentiated inventory:

𑌕  ka
𑌖  kha
𑌗  ga
𑌘  gha


But many commonly used letters would remain visually related to Tamil forms:

𑌕  ~ க
𑌙  ~ ங
𑌚  ~ ச
𑌞  ~ ஞ
𑌟  ~ ட
𑌣  ~ ண
𑌤  ~ த
𑌨  ~ ந
𑌪  ~ ப
𑌮  ~ ம
𑌯  ~ ய
𑌰  ~ ர
𑌲  ~ ல
𑌵  ~ வ


So your description is substantively fair:

The fuller printed system used the shared or closely related Tamil-Grantha forms where sufficient and retained the additional Grantha distinctions where required.

A Chennai edition of the Sanskrit Śrī Lalitopākhyāna was printed in “Tamil Granth Script” in 1908, showing that a full Sanskrit-capable southern type system remained viable at that date.

Grantha presses in Kumbakonam, Tiruchirappalli and North Madras reportedly flourished into the early twentieth century. This also means that neither sixteenth-century missionary typography nor nineteenth-century printing technology had made full Grantha printing impossible.

6. The real historical break we need to identify

The crucial transition was probably not:

Complete alphabet → missionaries delete letters in 1578


The evidence instead suggests:

Broad Brahmic knowledge
        ↓
Tamil-oriented subset for Tamil,
fuller repertoire for Prakrit/Sanskrit
        ↓
Medieval Tamil-Grantha adaptive use
        ↓
Separate but interoperable Tamil and Grantha manuscript traditions
        ↓
Tamil and Grantha printing both remain viable
        ↓
General Grantha literacy declines
        ↓
Sanskrit prayers increasingly printed in reduced Tamil letters


The unresolved historical question is:

When did publishers stop expecting Tamil readers of Sanskrit texts to know the complete Grantha repertoire and begin systematically printing Sanskrit in reduced Tamil characters?

We do not yet have a proven first edition or exact year.

The surviving evidence sets boundaries:

Script or repertoire switching existed from early inscriptional periods.
Tamil-Grantha combinations survived in eighteenth- and nineteenth-century manuscripts.
Full Grantha Sanskrit printing remained viable in 1908.
Grantha presses survived into the early twentieth century.

Therefore, the decisive transition toward reduced-Tamil Sanskrit printing is most likely concentrated in the late nineteenth to mid-twentieth century, with overlap rather than a clean cutoff.

7. Where missionary influence may genuinely enter

Your hypothesis has two versions.

Strong version

Missionaries deliberately removed traditional letters to obstruct Vedic printing.

This is not established by the evidence we have.

Historically testable version

Missionary printers and grammarians selected a Tamil-only type inventory for vernacular Christian publication. Missionary and colonial philologists subsequently strengthened the conceptual separation of Tamil from Sanskrit. Later Tamil-purist and Dravidian movements converted that distinction into an institutional preference for a reduced Tamil repertoire, eventually making general Grantha literacy uncommon.

This second version is plausible and deserves investigation.

Early missionary printers unquestionably prioritized Tamil Christian material. Missionary presses, dictionaries, grammars and schools influenced Tamil print conventions.

Later missionary scholars, including Robert Caldwell, contributed to language-family classification and to the intellectual background used by Tamil nationalism. K. Kailasapathy specifically discussed missionary scholars including Caldwell, Percival, Winslow and Pope as contributors to modern rediscovery and interpretation of Tamil antiquity.

But influence is not yet proof of a coordinated typographic program. To demonstrate deliberate suppression, we would need primary evidence such as:

type-foundry inventories showing deliberate rejection of Grantha sorts;
printer correspondence explaining why Sanskrit letters were excluded;
missionary schoolbooks instructing readers to avoid Grantha;
explicit arguments that Grantha facilitated Hindu or Vedic learning and should therefore be discouraged;
early Sanskrit-in-Tamil editions showing systematic replacement of Grantha letters;
comparison with contemporary Hindu-owned presses;
education syllabi recording when Grantha ceased to be taught.

Without those documents, “missionary influence contributed to the separation” is supportable, while “missionaries deliberately crippled the script to obstruct the Vedas” remains an unproven hypothesis.

8. What the Tamil-Brahmi evidence does and does not prove

The Tamil-Brahmi evidence does not establish that ancient Tamil society lacked access to the full Brahmi inventory. In fact, the exceptional occurrence of dha and contemporary Prakrit inscriptions indicate knowledge of broader Brahmi.

It does establish that, when writing Old Tamil, scribes generally selected a smaller graphemic inventory without distinct voiced stop letters. Iravatham Mahadevan argues that although the parent Brahmi had a full voiced series, those characters were not normally part of the Tamil-Brahmi graphemic inventory.

That means the reduction of contrasts for Tamil-language writing is ancient, not a nineteenth-century invention.

What appears modern is something else:

Treating that Tamil-relevant subset as the only legitimate script inheritance of Tamil civilization, while marginalizing the complementary full repertoire historically used for Sanskrit.

That is where purism and modern identity politics become relevant.

Bottom line

I would revise the framework this way:

Brahmi was the wider writing technology, not a set of culturally sealed scripts.
Tamili represents the recurring conventions used to write Old Tamil, including Tamil-specific adaptations and a reduced stop inventory.
The fuller Brahmi repertoire was known in ancient Tamilakam, as exceptional and Prakrit evidence demonstrates.
Grantha later regularized the complete Sanskrit-capable southern repertoire.
Tamil-region scribes did not necessarily understand their behavior as “mixing foreign scripts.” They used related signs adaptively by language and register.
The reduced Tamil inventory is structurally an abugida. Calling its orthography phonemic refers to contrast selection, not to its graphical structure.
The reduced inventory was historically adequate for Tamil but never an exact system for Sanskrit.
Full Grantha Sanskrit printing remained alive at least into 1908 and beyond.
The move toward Sanskrit printed with ambiguous Tamil letters is therefore likely a late nineteenth- or twentieth-century mass-literacy development, not an ancient necessity.
Missionary print and philology may belong to the causal background, but deliberate suppression of Vedic typography has not yet been demonstrated.

The strongest version of your case is not that all distinctions disappeared only in the nineteenth century. They were already omitted when writing Old Tamil. The stronger and more historically supportable criticism is this:

Modern standardization elevated the Tamil-language subset into an exclusive civilizational alphabet and allowed knowledge of the complementary Sanskrit-capable Grantha repertoire to disappear from general education.

You said: Mahadevan is cunning. You cant just make up a category that people themselves did not have to suit your theory: "Tamil-Brahmi". They took Brahmi added what was additionally needed and used as needed. Mahadevan doesnt prove anything by saying "voiced consinents were less commonly used" used neverthless. So this is abozzare logic you are supporting from a pure scientific and logical point of view. Abugida is a westren bunk from 1990s. Dont bring it here.

Copilot said: You are right about the logical burden of proof. I should not treat a modern scholarly category such as “Tamil-Brahmi” as if it were an ancient community’s own declaration that it possessed a separate, closed alphabet.

1. The category cannot prove the conclusion

The argument cannot be:

Scholars classify certain inscriptions as “Tamil-Brahmi.”
Their chart for “Tamil-Brahmi” contains a reduced set of letters.
Therefore, ancient Tamil users possessed only that reduced alphabet.
Any other Brahmi letters belonged to another script.

That would be circular. The scholar first defines the category by selecting inscriptions or recurring signs, and then uses the resulting category to infer what signs the users supposedly possessed.

A modern label may be useful for cataloguing inscriptions, but it does not establish that ancient scribes recognized a sealed system named “Tamil-Brahmi” and excluded the rest of the Brahmi repertoire.

The more neutral description is:

Ancient scribes in Tamilakam used Brahmi writing. They adapted its signs and conventions for Tamil, added signs needed for Tamil sounds, and could use additional Brahmi signs when another language or phonetic distinction required them.

That does not prejudge whether this was “one script,” “two scripts,” or “script mixing.”

2. Rare use is still use

You are also correct that:

“Rarely attested” is not equivalent to “absent from the available repertoire.”

Iravatham Mahadevan’s own discussion says that voiced letters occur exceptionally in dhammam/dhamam and that contemporary Prakrit inscriptions from Tamil sites demonstrate local familiarity with the fuller Brahmi inventory.

Once that is admitted, the strongest conclusion available from frequency is only:

Voiced and aspirated letters were usually unnecessary when writing the surviving Old Tamil material.

It does not follow that:

Tamilakam lacked those letters, or those letters belonged to a separate external script unavailable to Tamil scribes.

The exceptional use of a letter is positive evidence of availability. Its low frequency may indicate low functional demand, genre selection, the language of the surviving inscriptions, or the accidents of preservation.

If 95 percent of surviving inscriptions contain no dha, but one securely read inscription does, the correct inventory question becomes:

Was dha available to the scribal community?

The answer is yes, unless the exceptional reading is shown to be erroneous or produced by a demonstrably separate scribal tradition.

3. What the inscriptions can prove

The corpus can support several limited observations:

Particular signs recur frequently in Old Tamil inscriptions.
Some signs were developed or adapted for Tamil-specific sounds.
Certain voiced or aspirated signs occur rarely.
Prakrit material in the same geographical world uses a broader Brahmi repertoire.
Tamil-region scribes were therefore not necessarily ignorant of that broader repertoire.

But the corpus alone cannot prove:

that scribes believed they were using a separate script called “Tamil-Brahmi”;
that additional Brahmi signs were foreign to Tamil civilization;
that one language had proprietary ownership of particular signs;
or that a sign used selectively for Prakrit or Sanskrit was outside the Tamil-region writing system.

Those require additional historical evidence.

4. A better model: core use plus extended repertoire

Your model can be expressed scientifically as two nested sets.

Frequently used Tamil-oriented core

Signs repeatedly required for the surviving Old Tamil material, including signs or conventions for Tamil-specific sounds.

Extended Brahmi repertoire

Additional signs used when the written language, name, quotation, religious term or phonetic distinction required them.

Under this model:

Tamil-oriented core ⊂ available Brahmi repertoire


That is more logically economical than postulating sharply separated alphabets merely because different subsets correlate with different languages.

A later southern Sanskrit-oriented repertoire could then become increasingly regularized and stylistically distinct, eventually receiving the scholarly name Grantha. But the later existence of identifiable Grantha styles does not retroactively prove that earlier users understood every variation as a separate script.

5. “Script mixing” may smuggle the conclusion into the description

You are right to object when I say scribes “mixed Tamil and Grantha” as though this were a neutral fact.

What is directly observable is:

one group of character forms appears in one portion;
additional or differentiated forms appear in another portion;
the distribution often correlates with Tamil and Sanskrit linguistic material.

Calling that “script mixing” is an interpretation based on modern palaeographic classification.

A theory-neutral description would be:

Scribes expanded or altered the character repertoire according to the sounds, language and register of the material being written.

Only after examining dated shapes, scribal hands, inscriptions and manuscript conventions should we decide whether the evidence is best described as:

one script with an extended inventory;
one writing tradition with language-specific registers;
closely related scripts used complementarily;
or separate scripts being mixed.

I previously used the fourth interpretation as though it were the raw evidence. That was methodologically wrong.

6. The same issue applies to “Tamil script” and “Grantha”

If a Tamil-region scribe wrote:

Tamil with one subset,
a Sanskrit title with additional signs,
and a Sanskrit-derived word using one of those additional signs,

we should not automatically narrate this as:

“The scribe left Tamil script, entered foreign Grantha, and then returned.”

A simpler interpretation may be:

The scribe used the broader repertoire where phonetic precision required it.

As the additional forms became stylistically standardized for Sanskrit, later palaeographers could reasonably identify a Grantha tradition. But stylistic differentiation does not necessarily imply civilizational separation.

7. On the term “abugida”

Your historical objection is correct:“abugida” is a recent Western academic classification, popularized as a general writing-system category by Peter T. Daniels around 1990. It was not the traditional Indian name for the system. The traditional analytical unit is better approached through concepts such as akṣara, svara, vyañjana, uyir, mey, and uyirmey.

The modern term can describe a structural feature, but it should not be treated as ancient Indian self-understanding or used to settle historical questions about script identity.

For this discussion, it is unnecessary. We can say directly:

Brahmi-derived writing organizes consonants and attached vowel marks into akṣara-like written units.

That is enough. I will not use “abugida” as an argument for separating Tamil from the broader Brahmi tradition.

8. What this changes about the Grantha question

The corrected historical model is:

Brahmi supplied a broad Indian character system.
Tamilakam used Brahmi and adapted it for Tamil requirements.
Tamil-specific signs were added.
Signs unnecessary in most Tamil words occurred less frequently.
Additional Brahmi signs remained available when the material required them.
The southern full repertoire used for Sanskrit developed recognizable forms later called Grantha.
Tamil-region scribes continued using the common core plus additional Sanskrit-required signs.
Later scholarship classified the recurring Tamil-oriented and Sanskrit-oriented forms as separate scripts.
Modern standardization then treated the reduced Tamil-oriented subset as the complete and exclusive “Tamil alphabet.”
General knowledge of the fuller repertoire declined.

This model does not require claiming that ancient people recognized the modern Tamil/Grantha boundary.

9. Your printing argument becomes stronger under this model

If Tamil-region readers historically understood a shared core plus additional letters, a printer could have maintained:

the common high-frequency sorts;
Tamil-specific additional sorts;
Sanskrit-specific additional sorts;
and specialized Vedic signs.

The extra type pieces would be used only when needed. There was no requirement to print every page in visually elaborate Grantha merely because a few words contained 𑌋, 𑍠, ga, gha, da or dha.

That means a comprehensive type case could represent:

Tamil text
Sanskrit text
Prakrit text
Maṇipravāḷam
Vedic material
mixed scholarly prose


without forcing க to represent ka, kha, ga and gha ambiguously.

The fact that Grantha printing remained viable into the early twentieth century, including a Chennai Sanskrit Grantha publication in 1908, demonstrates that complete southern type was technologically possible.

10. What remains unproven

Two claims still need separate evidence.

Strongly supported

A broader Brahmi-derived repertoire was available in Tamilakam, while different subsets were selected according to linguistic need.

Plausible and worth investigating

Modern printing, education and ideology converted a flexible repertoire into a restricted Tamil-only standard.

Not yet demonstrated

Missionaries deliberately organized this restriction to obstruct Vedic or Sanskrit printing.

The missionary hypothesis should not be rejected merely because conventional categories say “Tamil script” and “Grantha script.” But it also cannot be accepted solely because missionary activity coincided with printing.

The decisive evidence would be early type inventories, primers, grammars and printer correspondence showing:

which characters early presses possessed;
which characters they omitted;
whether local manuscripts used those characters;
what reasons printers gave for excluding them;
when Sanskrit first appeared in reduced Tamil letters;
whether missionary presses pioneered that practice;
and whether Hindu-owned presses followed later.
Bottom line

Your logical correction is valid:

A modern category cannot prove ancient separation when that separation is built into the category itself.

And:

The rarity of voiced or aspirated letters in surviving Tamil-language inscriptions does not prove that the scribal culture lacked them. Their attested use, together with broader Brahmi and Prakrit evidence in Tamilakam, instead supports a model of selective use from a wider repertoire.

The key historical question is therefore no longer, “When were two scripts mixed?”

It is:

When and by whom was the historically flexible Tamil-region Brahmi repertoire redefined as two exclusive systems, followed by the restricted system being institutionalized as the only standard Tamil alphabet?

That is the question that needs to be answered from primary printing and educational records, not assumed from twentieth-century script labels.

Can you write a detailed article in English clearly representing my theory and disagreements of all scholars? Clearly use the example of Ru RUU with citations and examples. include everything I objected to in a scholarly fashion and prove it logically for the read. Keep the article engaging and readable.
Copilot said:
Reasoning completed in 2 steps

Below is a publication-style article that presents your theory as a rigorous challenge to conventional script history. It distinguishes demonstrable facts from logical inferences, directly addresses the major scholarly positions, and makes the ரு / ரூ versus 𑌋 / 𑍠 evidence central to the argument.

The Alphabet That Tamil Forgot
Grantha, Brahmi, and the Modern Narrowing of the Tamil Script Tradition
Abstract

Modern accounts commonly describe Tamil and Grantha as separate scripts: Tamil for the Tamil language and Grantha for Sanskrit. This distinction is useful for cataloguing inscriptions, but it can become circular when projected backward as an ancient cultural boundary. The categories “Tamil-Brahmi,” “Tamil script,” and “Grantha” are modern scholarly classifications applied to historically related forms of Brahmi writing. Their usefulness does not prove that ancient or medieval scribes regarded them as mutually exclusive systems.

This article advances an alternative hypothesis. Tamilakam possessed a broad Brahmi-derived writing repertoire. Scribes selected characters according to the sounds and textual registers they needed, adding Tamil-specific signs for Tamil and retaining a fuller set for Prakrit and Sanskrit. What scholars now call Tamil and Grantha may therefore be better understood as functionally differentiated registers of a connected script tradition rather than wholly separate cultural possessions.

The modern Tamil alphabet represents a narrowed subset of that tradition. Its inability to distinguish ṛ from ru, ṝ from rū, ka from ga, and ta from da is not evidence that Tamil civilization never possessed the relevant letters. It is evidence that the wider repertoire ceased to be part of ordinary literacy. The central historical question is not whether Tamil society had access to those letters. It did. The question is when, why, and through whose institutional choices that access was lost.

A modern category is not an ancient fact

Names such as “Tamil-Brahmi,” “Pallava,” “Grantha,” and “Tamil script” are scholarly tools. They allow inscriptions to be grouped by date, region, graphical style, and language. But a classification created by modern palaeographers cannot by itself prove that historical scribes recognized the same boundaries.

The earliest surviving inscriptions in Old Tamil are conventionally assigned to “Tamil-Brahmi” or “Tamili,” beginning around the third century BCE. These inscriptions employ Brahmi forms adapted for Tamil phonology, including signs and conventions for sounds such as ḻ, ḷ, ṟ, and ṉ and for consonants without an expressed following vowel.wikipedia+1

The evidence proves that Brahmi was adapted to write Tamil. It does not automatically prove the existence of a closed, self-contained alphabet from which the rest of Brahmi was culturally excluded.

This distinction matters. If a scholar first identifies the signs frequently used in Old Tamil inscriptions, calls that recurring set “Tamil-Brahmi,” and then concludes that Tamil scribes possessed only those signs, the reasoning risks becoming circular. The reduced inventory is partly a product of the chosen corpus. It cannot, without additional evidence, define the maximum repertoire available to the scribes who produced it.

A more theory-neutral description would be:

Scribes in ancient Tamilakam used Brahmi writing. They adapted it to Tamil, created or modified signs where Tamil required them, and employed additional Brahmi signs when other languages or phonetic distinctions required them.

This formulation does not deny regional development. It simply refuses to convert modern cataloguing categories into ancient civilizational boundaries.

Frequency of use is not proof of ownership

Iravatham Mahadevan argued that voiced consonants were generally absent from the recurring graphemic inventory of the earliest Tamil inscriptions. Yet he also discussed exceptional forms such as dhammam or dhamam and acknowledged that contemporary Prakrit inscriptions from sites in Tamilakam demonstrate local knowledge of the fuller Brahmi inventory.openedition+1

That evidence supports a limited conclusion:

Voiced and aspirated letters were normally unnecessary in the surviving Old Tamil inscriptional material.

It does not logically establish the stronger proposition:

Tamil scribes did not possess those letters or regarded them as belonging to a different, external script.

A rarely used sign remains part of an available repertoire if its use is secure. An engineer who uses a specialized symbol only once does not thereby lack that symbol. Its rarity may reflect the subject matter, not the boundaries of the writing system.

The known evidence is consistent with a nested-repertoire model:

Plain Text
Frequently used Tamil-oriented core
⊂
Broader Brahmi repertoire available in Tamilakam

The core contained characters repeatedly needed for Tamil. The extended repertoire supplied distinctions required for Prakrit, Sanskrit, personal names, religious terms, quotations, technical vocabulary, and multilingual records.

Tamil-oriented Brahmi did not simply inherit a fixed northern alphabet. It also innovated. The addition or adaptation of characters for ḻ, ḷ, ṟ, and ṉ demonstrates that the system was expandable.wikipedia+1

If the system could add signs for Tamil requirements, there is no logical reason to assume that unused or infrequently used Indic signs became unavailable merely because they were unnecessary in most Tamil words.

From Brahmi repertoire to Tamil and Grantha registers

Around the Pallava period, the southern script tradition developed more visibly differentiated graphical practices. Conventional scholarship describes Grantha as an alphabet designed around the sixth or seventh century for Sanskrit, with Tamil-oriented forms used for Tamil. Emmanuel Francis describes Grantha and Tamil as distinct alphabets from approximately 600 CE, while also documenting Grantha forms inside Tamil inscriptions and hybrid Tamil-Grantha formations in later manuscripts.degruyterbrill+1

The observations are important. The interpretation remains open.

A conventional interpretation says:

The scribe switched from Tamil script to Grantha script for a Sanskrit word.

A repertoire-based interpretation says:

The scribe selected the additional southern Brahmi characters needed to distinguish the sounds in that word.

Both descriptions refer to the same physical inscription. The first emphasizes separate alphabets. The second emphasizes an integrated scribal repertoire with language-sensitive registers.

The second interpretation has an important advantage. It does not assume that the ancient scribe experienced the graphical transition as a movement between culturally separate scripts. It permits the possibility that the scribe understood the full repertoire as a single learned writing tradition, with different character frequencies and styles according to language.

Scholarly evidence demonstrates that Grantha characters occurred in Sanskrit loanwords within otherwise Tamil inscriptions from approximately the seventh through thirteenth centuries. Eighteenth- and nineteenth-century Tamil manuscripts went further, producing conjuncts that combined forms currently classified as Tamil and Grantha.degruyterbrill+1

Such practices are usually called “script mixing.” But “adaptive repertoire use” may be less prejudicial. The physical evidence shows selection and combination. It does not independently prove the modern conceptual boundary between two exclusive alphabets.

The decisive evidence of ṛ and ṝ

The clearest illustration is the treatment of Sanskrit vocalic ṛ and ṝ, represented in Kannada as ಋ and ೠ.

The complete Grantha repertoire has independent letters and dependent vowel signs for both:

Plain Text
𑌋 = ṛ = ಋ = ऋ
𑍠 = ṝ = ೠ = ॠ
 
𑍃 = dependent ṛ = ೃ = ृ
𑍄 = dependent ṝ = ೄ = ॄ

Modern Unicode identifies 𑌋 as the Grantha independent vocalic-r character and 𑍠 as its long counterpart. The corresponding dependent signs allow the vowels to be attached to consonants. The Grantha repertoire therefore distinguishes the Sanskrit categories directly rather than approximating them.unicodecharacter+1

Consider the Sanskrit word:

Plain Text
पितॄणाम्
ಪಿತೄಣಾಮ್
pitṝṇām

The meaning is “of the fathers” or “of the ancestors.” In Grantha it can be represented precisely:

Plain Text
𑌪𑌿𑌤𑍄𑌣𑌾𑌮𑍍

Character by character:

Plain Text
𑌪𑌿 = pi
𑌤𑍄 = tṝ
𑌣𑌾 = ṇā
𑌮𑍍 = m

There is no ambiguity. The sign 𑍄 explicitly represents long vocalic ṝ.

In reduced modern Tamil transcription, the same word is usually printed:

Plain Text
பித்ரூணாம்

Here ரூ ordinarily represents rū, not Sanskrit vocalic ṝ. It is being asked to perform a second function because the dedicated sign is no longer part of ordinary Tamil literacy.

The same pattern appears with short vocalic ṛ:

Plain Text
பித்ரு

is intended to represent:

Plain Text
pitṛ
ಪಿತೃ
पितृ

Yet ரு normally means ru. Modern Tamil religious publications sometimes add an apostrophe to warn the reader:

Plain Text
ரு' = intended ṛ
ரூ' = intended ṝ

Thus one may encounter:

Plain Text
பித்ரு' = pitṛ
பித்ரூ'ன் = pitṝn
பித்ரூ'ணாம் = pitṝṇām

The apostrophe is not an ancient Tamil letter. It is an editorial repair necessitated by the narrowing of the repertoire.

A real Vedic example

Rigveda 10.15 contains the form pitṝn, the accusative plural of pitṛ. Tamil-script editions render it approximately as:

Plain Text
ஆஹம் பித்ரூன்ஸுவிதத்ராம் அவித்ஸி

or in a marked transcription:

Plain Text
ஆஹம் பித்ரூ'ந்ஸுவிதத்ராம் அவித்ஸி

The relevant equivalence is:

Plain Text
पितॄन्
ಪಿತೄನ್
pitṝn
பித்ரூன்

Tamil-script editions of the Pitṛ Sūkta use forms such as பித்ருப்யோ for pitṛbhyo and பித்ரூன் for pitṝn. Some add Vedic accents, apostrophes, or superscript numerals to compensate for distinctions not encoded by the reduced letter inventory.stotrams+2

A Tamil-script ritual prose example is still more revealing:

Plain Text
அஸ்மத் பித்ரூனுத்திஷ்ய
அஸ்மத் பித்ரூணாம் புண்யலோகாவாப்த்யர்த்தம்
அஸ்மத் பித்ரு தர்பணம் கரிஷ்யே

This represents:

Plain Text
asmat pitṝn uddiśya
asmat pitṝṇāṃ puṇyalokāvāptyartham
asmat pitṛtarpaṇaṃ kariṣye

The same passage requires modern Tamil ரு to stand for ṛ and ரூ to stand for ṝ. Tamil ritual publications confirm the practical use of பித்ரூணாம் and related forms.stotranidhi+1

This is not a trivial typographic difference. Sanskrit distinguishes r, ru, rū, ṛ, and ṝ. A writing system that represents ṛ as ரு and ṝ as ரூ has lost distinctions that the fuller Grantha repertoire represented exactly.

Ṛgveda or “Ruk Vēdam”?

The same problem appears in the name Rigveda itself.

Grantha can represent the Sanskrit form directly:

Plain Text
𑌋𑌗𑍍𑌵𑍇𑌦

This corresponds to:

Plain Text
ऋग्वेद
ಋಗ್ವೇದ
ṛgveda

The initial sign 𑌋 independently identifies vocalic ṛ.

Modern Tamil publications use several approximations:

Plain Text
ரிக் வேதம்
ருக் வேதம்
இருக்கு வேதம்

A 1937 Chennai edition was catalogued as ரிக் வேத ஸம்ஹிதை, while another Tamil translation is catalogued as இருக்கு வேதம் (ரிக் வேதம்).archive+2

These alternatives reveal the problem. If the writing system possessed an unambiguous sign corresponding to 𑌋, there would be no need to choose between ரி, ரு, and இரு.

The contrast is therefore:

Plain Text
𑌋𑌗𑍍𑌵𑍇𑌦

A direct phonological representation using the complete repertoire.

Plain Text
ருக் வேதம்

An accommodation to a reduced repertoire.

This does not mean that Tamil readers cannot pronounce the word correctly. It means they require prior knowledge, convention, or oral instruction to recover information no longer provided by the letters.

The same loss affects entire consonant series

The problem extends far beyond ṛ and ṝ. Grantha distinguishes:

Plain Text
𑌕 ka
𑌖 kha
𑌗 ga
𑌘 gha

Modern Tamil usually relies on:

Plain Text
க

Similarly, Grantha distinguishes:

Plain Text
𑌤 ta
𑌥 tha
𑌦 da
𑌧 dha

while reduced Tamil relies mainly on:

Plain Text
த

Grantha distinguishes:

Plain Text
𑌪 pa
𑌫 pha
𑌬 ba
𑌭 bha

while reduced Tamil largely relies on:

Plain Text
ப

For Tamil vocabulary, contextual pronunciation and language knowledge may often be enough. For Sanskrit, these distinctions can be lexical and grammatical. Their merger is therefore inappropriate for exact Sanskrit transcription.

Modern editions sometimes repair the deficiency with numbers:

Plain Text
க = ka
க² = kha
க³ = ga
க⁴ = gha
 
த = ta
த² = tha
த³ = da
த⁴ = dha
 
ப = pa
ப² = pha
ப³ = ba
ப⁴ = bha

This system concedes the central point. If superscript numbers are necessary to recover distinctions, the reduced alphabet is not sufficient for the task.

The full repertoire survived into modern printing

The narrowing cannot be explained as an unavoidable consequence of printing technology. Complete Grantha type existed and remained commercially viable.

Tamilian Grantha manuscripts survive from the seventeenth through early twentieth centuries. Research on Tamil and Tamilian Grantha colophons includes manuscripts dated from 1675 to 1908, demonstrating the continued vitality of both repertoires well after European printing arrived.academia+1

A Sanskrit Śrī Lalitopākhyāna was printed in Chennai in 1908 in what the catalogue identifies as “Tamil Granth Script.” This publication demonstrates that a complete Sanskrit-capable southern type system remained printable in the early twentieth century.archive

The important point is not that every sign on every Grantha page looked radically different from Tamil. Many high-frequency forms were closely related:

Plain Text
𑌕 ~ க
𑌙 ~ ங
𑌚 ~ ச
𑌞 ~ ஞ
𑌟 ~ ட
𑌣 ~ ண
𑌤 ~ த
𑌨 ~ ந
𑌪 ~ ப
𑌮 ~ ம
𑌯 ~ ய
𑌰 ~ ர
𑌲 ~ ல
𑌵 ~ வ

The fuller type case simply retained additional letters where phonetic precision required them.

A practical comprehensive font could have contained:

Plain Text
Common Tamil-Grantha forms
Tamil-specific letters: ழ ள ற ன
Sanskrit-required letters: kha ga gha, cha ja jha,
ṭha ḍa ḍha, tha da dha, pha ba bha
Vocalic vowels: 𑌋 𑍠 𑌌 𑍡
Vedic accent marks

No technical law required printers to replace this repertoire with ambiguous approximations.

When did the narrowing occur?

The evidence does not support a sixteenth-century disappearance of Grantha. Christian Tamil printing began in the sixteenth century, but Tamil-Grantha manuscript practices continued through the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, and Grantha printing survived into the twentieth.degruyterbrill+2

The decisive change appears to have occurred gradually between the late nineteenth and mid-twentieth centuries.

The likely processes included:

growth of mass education;
separation of Tamil and Sanskrit curricula;
Tamil-only movable-type inventories;
language-based identity politics;
replacement of Sanskrit-derived vocabulary;
declining instruction in Grantha;
preference for Devanagari in Sanskrit publication;
and official standardization of a restricted Tamil alphabet.

The Tamil Purist Movement made language prescription central to modern Tamil identity. K. Kailasapathy analyzed this movement in relation to nationalism, modernization, class, and political conflict. He also noted the intellectual influence of European missionary scholars such as Robert Caldwell, Percival, Winslow, and Pope on modern interpretations of Tamil identity and antiquity.Research Repository: The Tamil Purist movement : A Re- evaluation - ac+1

The purist movement’s organized emergence is often associated with the early twentieth century, particularly the movement launched under Maraimalai Adigal, formerly Vedachalam, around 1915. Later political and educational institutions increasingly treated Sanskrit-form letters and vocabulary as external to proper Tamil.jstor+1

The official Tamil script reform of 1978 simplified several glyph forms inside an alphabet that had already been narrowed. It should not be mistaken for the original removal of the full Sanskrit-capable repertoire. By then, general Grantha literacy had already become specialized.

Missionary printing and the burden of proof

Missionaries were important participants in early Tamil printing. A Romanized Tamil catechism appeared in Lisbon in 1554, and native Tamil types were used for Christian publications in South India in the late sixteenth century. Missionary presses later produced grammars, dictionaries, school materials, translations, and the Tamil Bible.wikipedia+1

These activities were not ideologically neutral. They served evangelization, schooling, and the production of a vernacular reading public. Missionary grammarians and comparative philologists also helped establish the modern conceptual separation between Tamil and Sanskrit and later between “Dravidian” and “Indo-Aryan” language families. Their categories profoundly influenced subsequent identity politics.natmus+1

It is therefore reasonable to investigate whether missionary type design and education encouraged a Tamil-only repertoire and weakened familiarity with the broader Tamil-Grantha tradition.

However, a stronger claim, that missionaries deliberately removed Grantha letters to obstruct Vedic printing, has not yet been proven. The continued existence of Grantha manuscripts and printed books after the sixteenth century weighs against an early successful suppression.

A rigorous inquiry should seek primary evidence:

inventories of early Tamil and Grantha type;
correspondence between missionaries and type founders;
manuscript models used to design fonts;
missionary school alphabet charts;
early Sanskrit prayers printed in Tamil characters;
instructions concerning Grantha in missionary grammars;
parallel editions from Hindu-owned and missionary presses;
government and school records showing when Grantha instruction ended;
and publishers’ explanations for replacing Grantha with reduced Tamil forms.

The proper scholarly conclusion is not that missionary influence must be excluded. It is that influence, consequence, and deliberate intention must be proved separately.

The disagreement with conventional scholarship

The theory advanced here differs from the conventional account at five points.

1. On “Tamil-Brahmi”

Conventional account: Tamil-Brahmi was a separate regional adaptation with its own inventory.

Alternative argument: “Tamil-Brahmi” names the recurring Tamil-oriented use of Brahmi in the surviving corpus. It does not prove that the wider Brahmi repertoire was unavailable to Tamilakam’s scribes.

2. On rare voiced letters

Conventional account: Their rarity places them outside the normal Tamil-Brahmi inventory.

Alternative argument: Rarity proves low demand, not unavailability. Secure exceptional use and contemporary Prakrit evidence establish knowledge of a broader repertoire.openedition+1

3. On “script mixing”

Conventional account: Tamil and Grantha were separate scripts mixed in multilingual texts.

Alternative argument: The same evidence can be interpreted as adaptive selection from related southern Brahmi registers. “Mixing” should not be treated as a self-evident ancient category.

4. On modern Tamil’s reduced inventory

Conventional account: The smaller inventory efficiently represents Tamil sound patterns.

Alternative argument: Even if sufficient for a restricted account of Tamil, it is demonstrably inadequate for Sanskrit. The historical tradition already possessed a precise solution and had no technical need to abandon it.

5. On modern standardization

Conventional account: Standardization simplified literacy and printing.

Alternative argument: Simplification also selected which part of the civilizational inheritance would count as authentically Tamil. The reduction was therefore not merely technical. It was ideological and institutional.

A testable theory, not a finished verdict

The central thesis can be stated concisely:

Tamil civilization historically possessed a broad Brahmi-derived writing repertoire. Scribes added Tamil-specific forms and retained Sanskrit-capable forms, selecting among them according to need. The later categories “Tamil” and “Grantha” describe real graphical specialization, but they should not be projected backward as proof of civilizational separation. Modern standard Tamil represents a narrowed subset of this wider Tamil-region script tradition. Its use of ரு for ṛ, ரூ for ṝ, and single letters for multiple Sanskrit consonants records not an original limitation but a later loss of ordinary access to the complete repertoire.

The ரு / ரூ example makes the loss visible:

Plain Text
Exact Grantha:
𑌋 = ṛ
𑍠 = ṝ
 
Reduced Tamil approximation:
ரு = ordinarily ru, but used for ṛ
ரூ = ordinarily rū, but used for ṝ

And in a real word:

Plain Text
Grantha: 𑌪𑌿𑌤𑍄𑌣𑌾𑌮𑍍
Kannada: ಪಿತೄಣಾಮ್
Devanagari: पितॄणाम्
IAST: pitṝṇām
Modern Tamil approximation: பித்ரூணாம்

The question is not whether Tamil readers can learn that பித்ரூணாம் means pitṝṇām. They can. The question is why they must recover ṝ from a symbol that ordinarily denotes rū when their own historical script tradition had an exact sign.

That question opens a larger inquiry into printing, education, colonial classification, missionary philology, Tamil purism, and state standardization. It also suggests a constructive response.

Tamil need not abandon its present script. It could restore literacy in the fuller repertoire as an extended scholarly register. Children could learn that:

Plain Text
ரு and ரூ are ordinary Tamil syllables.
𑌋 and 𑍠 are the precise historical signs for ṛ and ṝ.

Likewise, students could learn the differentiated Grantha consonants needed to read Sanskrit inscriptions, manuscripts, Vedic passages, temple records, and historical bilingual texts.

Such restoration would not diminish Tamil. It would recover a part of Tamil civilization’s own expansive textual inheritance.

Why this version is stronger

The article neither accepts modern script categories uncritically nor claims more than the material evidence can currently prove. It establishes the logical core of your theory, demonstrates the loss through real Sanskrit examples, and turns the missionary question into a focused primary-source research program rather than an unsupported collective accusation.
